<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

An Egyptian-fraction expansion of a positive rational number is a finite sum of distinct reciprocals of positive integers. For integers $1\le a<b$, let $N(a,b)$ be the least $k$ for which $$\frac ab=\frac1{n_1}+\cdots+\frac1{n_k},
 \qquad 2\le n_1<\cdots<n_k,\qquad n_i\in\mathbb Z,$$ and put $N(b)=\max_{1\le a<b}N(a,b)$. The fractions $a/b$ need not be in lowest terms, and the denominators $n_i$ have no prescribed upper bound. The greedy algorithm shows that such expansions exist. We determine the order of their maximum minimum length. Individual numerators can have much shorter expansions: for example, $N(1,b)=1$ for every $b\ge2$.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There are absolute constants $c_1,c_2>0$ and $b_0$ such that, for every integer $b\ge b_0$, $$c_1\log\log b\le N(b)\le c_2\log\log b.$$ In particular, the upper bound holds for every integer numerator $1\le a<b$.*

</div>

Nakayama’s study of $N(a,b)$ emphasized arithmetic criteria for expansions with few terms (Nakayama 1940, sec. I) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Nakayama1940](#ref-Nakayama1940)]. The uniform problem asks how these minimum lengths behave as the numerator varies over a fixed denominator. In 1950, Erdős reported de Bruijn’s bound $N(b)\ll\log b/\log\log\log b$ and improved it to $N(b)\ll\log b/\log\log b$ (Erdős 1950, 195, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Erdos1950](#ref-Erdos1950)]. He proposed the double-logarithmic upper bound and proved the matching lower order, already for the numerator $b-1$ (Erdős 1950, 195, Theorem 2) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Erdos1950](#ref-Erdos1950)]. The question appears again in Erdős and Graham (Erdős and Graham 1980, 28:37–38) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[ErdosGraham1980](#ref-ErdosGraham1980)] and is recorded as Erdős Problem 304 (Bloom, n.d.b). Vose subsequently obtained $N(b)\ll\sqrt{\log b}$ (Vose 1985) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Vose1985](#ref-Vose1985)]; an explicit modern statement of his uniform bound is given in (Doorn and Tang 2026, Lemma 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[vanDoornTang2026](#ref-vanDoornTang2026)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> proves Erdős’s conjecture affirmatively. The lower bound is classical; the issue is the uniform upper bound.

A related line of work controls the denominators as well as the length. Tenenbaum and Yokota proved that, for each fixed $\varepsilon>0$ and every sufficiently large $b$, every $a/b\in(0,1)$ has a distinct expansion of length at most $(1+\varepsilon)\log b/\log\log b$, with all denominators at most $4b(\log b)^2\log\log b$ (Tenenbaum and Yokota 1990, Theorem, p. 151) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[TenenbaumYokota1990](#ref-TenenbaumYokota1990)]. Their length estimate serves this simultaneous constraint. Here we optimize only the order of the length and leave the denominator sizes unrestricted.

The uniform theorem also determines the double-logarithmic order of the number of representations of one. For each positive integer $k$, put $$F(k)=\#\left\{(n_1,\ldots,n_k):
  1\le n_1<\cdots<n_k,\quad n_i\in\mathbb Z,\quad
  \frac1{n_1}+\cdots+\frac1{n_k}=1\right\}.$$ Here too there is no upper bound on the denominators.

<a id="stmt-cor-counting"></a>
<div id="cor:counting" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2**. *There are absolute constants $c,C>0$ and $k_0$ such that, for every integer $k\ge k_0$, $$ck\le\log\log F(k)\le Ck.$$*

</div>

Erdős and Graham asked for good estimates for this counting function (Erdős and Graham 1980, 28:32) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[ErdosGraham1980](#ref-ErdosGraham1980)]. Konyagin stated a lower bound with double logarithm of order $k/\log k$ (Konyagin 2014, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Konyagin2014](#ref-Konyagin2014)]; Elsholtz obtained this lower order even when every denominator is congruent to $1$ or $-1$ modulo a fixed squarefree integer $P>1$: the bound holds for every sufficiently large $k$, with $k$ required to be odd when $P$ is even, and its constant may depend on $P$ (Elsholtz 2016, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Elsholtz2016](#ref-Elsholtz2016)]. Elsholtz and Planitzer proved an upper bound with double logarithm $O(k)$, allowing repetitions as well (Elsholtz and Planitzer 2021, Corollary 3(2)) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[ElsholtzPlanitzer2021](#ref-ElsholtzPlanitzer2021)]. Corollary <a href="#cor:counting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:counting">2</a> determines the double-logarithmic order for unrestricted distinct denominators.

We can also ask which exact denominators occur among these expansions. For each integer $k\ge1$, let $D_k$ be the set of integers $m\ge2$ for which there are positive integers $n_1<\cdots<n_k$ satisfying $$1=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac1{n_i},\qquad
 m\in\{n_1,\ldots,n_k\}.$$ Set $$v(k)=\min\bigl(\{2,3,\ldots\}\setminus D_k\bigr).$$ The elementary denominator bound proved below makes $D_k$ finite, so this minimum exists. Each $m\in D_k$ may use a different expansion; membership requires the exact denominator $m$ and exactly $k$ distinct terms.

<a id="stmt-cor-prescribed"></a>
<div id="cor:prescribed" class="corollary">

**Corollary 3**. *For every sufficiently large integer $k$, $$\exp\bigl(\exp(k/600)\bigr)\le v(k)\le1+k^{2^{k-1}}.$$ More precisely, $$\frac{\log2}{257}
 \le\liminf_{k\to\infty}\frac{\log\log v(k)}k
 \le\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{\log\log v(k)}k
 \le\log2.$$ Equivalently for the lower bound, every fixed $c<\log2/257$ satisfies $v(k)\ge\exp(\exp(ck))$ for every sufficiently large integer $k$. In particular $\log\log v(k)=\Theta(k)$.*

</div>

The prescribed-denominator question originates in Erdős and Graham (Erdős and Graham 1980, 28:35) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[ErdosGraham1980](#ref-ErdosGraham1980)]; see also Problem 293 (Bloom, n.d.a). Van Doorn and Tang prove the lower bound $v(k)\ge\exp(c k^2)$ for an absolute $c>0$ (Doorn and Tang 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[vanDoornTang2026](#ref-vanDoornTang2026)]. They also show that an exact prescribed denominator can be retained while increasing the length of a distinct expansion (Doorn and Tang 2026, Lemma 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[vanDoornTang2026](#ref-vanDoornTang2026)] and anticipate the connection between uniform short expansions and a double-exponential lower bound for $v(k)$ (Doorn and Tang 2026, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[vanDoornTang2026](#ref-vanDoornTang2026)]. We give the required marker-avoidance argument below, thereby deriving the qualitative order from Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. A direct construction additionally gives the numerical lower slope in Corollary <a href="#cor:prescribed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:prescribed">3</a>, without quantifying the constant $c_2$ in the uniform theorem. The elementary upper bound is convenient rather than best known: sharper public upper bounds follow from the counting estimates of Elsholtz and Planitzer (Elsholtz and Planitzer 2021, Corollary 3(2)) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[ElsholtzPlanitzer2021](#ref-ElsholtzPlanitzer2021)].

The two consequences impose different requirements on a construction. To obtain many expansions, it suffices to retain a denominator divisible by an integer with many prime factors. To include a prescribed integer $m$ in $D_k$, the exact term $1/m$ must survive. The latter requirement governs both the initial construction and the operation that increases its length.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The proof in outline

Put $S=\log b$. After $O(\log S)$ greedy steps, either the expansion is complete or its remainder has the form $A/C$, where $A\le b$ and $C$ lies in a prescribed exponential range in $S$. We then construct an auxiliary integer $M$ with $e^S<M=\exp(O(S))$ and a large interval of numerators in which almost every fraction $u/(MC)$ has an expansion of length $O(\log S)$. This dense family suffices for the original numerator: a suitable integer multiple $gAM$ lies well inside the interval, and it is the sum of two numerators from that dense family. Adding their expansions and dividing by $g$ represents $A/C$.

The dense family is obtained by descending through $O(\log S)$ numerator ranges. At a range whose upper endpoint exceeds $e^S$, we represent fractions $u/(MC)$; at lower ranges we represent $u/M$, which is still less than one. Write $Q=C$ or $Q=1$ for the denominator factor in the current range. A divisor $t$ of $M$ gives the identity $$\frac{u}{MQ}
 =\frac1{(M/t)z}+\frac1z\frac{h}{MQ},
 \qquad z=\left\lceil\frac{Qt}{u}\right\rceil,
 \qquad h=uz-Qt.$$ Thus one unit fraction reduces the problem to the residue of $-Qt$ modulo $u$. When the denominator factor changes from $C$ to $1$, an expansion over $M$ can be transferred back over $MC$ by multiplying every denominator by $C$. We choose $M$ so that, for most numerators at each range, many of its divisors give small residues. Deterministic products of distinct primes provide the large-range estimate; independent random products provide the small-range estimate. At the bottom, the same construction gives a reduction for every remaining numerator, followed by a binary expansion.

The difficulty is to prevent the exceptional numerators from accumulating as these reductions are joined. For fixed $t$ and $h$, a predecessor $u$ must divide $Qt+h$. A uniform moment estimate for the number of small divisors in a shifted interval controls the total number of predecessors of a small exceptional set. Hölder’s inequality then gives a recurrence for the exceptional proportions that remains small throughout the descent. This combination of residue distribution, uniform divisor moments, and the final passage from a dense family to every numerator is the main mechanism of the proof.

The use of a common auxiliary denominator connects the proof to the classical divisor method. Erdős’s construction writes suitable integers as sums of distinct divisors of a factorial and turns those sums into unit fractions (Erdős 1950, sec. 1, pp. 199–203) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Erdos1950](#ref-Erdos1950)]. Tenenbaum and Yokota obtain a shorter divisor decomposition by choosing successive divisors close to the remaining numerator (Tenenbaum and Yokota 1990, Lemma 4 and Section 3) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[TenenbaumYokota1990](#ref-TenenbaumYokota1990)]. The present descent instead selects divisors through the residues they produce. Modular control by finite Fourier methods also occurs in Croot’s work (Croot 1999) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Croot1999](#ref-Croot1999)] and Martin’s development of it (Martin 2000, sec. 4, Lemmas 11–13) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Martin2000](#ref-Martin2000)], where subset sums of modular inverses remove large prime-power denominator factors. Here prime-product distributions supply many possible reductions, and a uniform divisor moment keeps their exceptional sets under control across all levels. The moment proof uses the prime-factor splitting method of Erdős (Erdős 1952, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Erdos1952Divisors](#ref-Erdos1952Divisors)]; the truncation and shift uniformity needed here are established below.

Section <a href="#sec:elementary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:elementary">2</a> isolates the dense-family statement and deduces Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> from it. Section <a href="#sec:divisors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:divisors">3</a> proves the divisor estimate, Section <a href="#sec:residues" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:residues">4</a> constructs the residue distributions, and Section <a href="#sec:descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:descent">5</a> completes the dense-family argument. The construction constants are fixed first, then a moment order large enough for the descent, and finally a sufficiently large lower bound on $S$. Section <a href="#sec:counting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:counting">6</a> then deduces Corollary <a href="#cor:counting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:counting">2</a>. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> supplies a short expansion containing a denominator with many prime factors. Splitting that denominator over its divisors gives many distinct expansions, and an injective padding operation gives every sufficiently large prescribed length. Divisor-rich denominators and divisor-indexed splitting appear in Konyagin and Elsholtz (Konyagin 2014; Elsholtz 2016) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Konyagin2014](#ref-Konyagin2014), [Elsholtz2016](#ref-Elsholtz2016)]; Konyagin explicitly records the padding injection (Konyagin 2014, 312) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Konyagin2014](#ref-Konyagin2014)].

For the prescribed-denominator consequence, Section <a href="#sec:prescribed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prescribed">7</a> reserves $1/m$ and uses a greedy prefix that skips denominator $m$. The remaining sum is smaller than every reserved reciprocal, so any positive expansion of that remainder avoids all reserved denominators. Applying Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> gives a short marked expansion, and a separate padding lemma retains the exact marker at every later length. For an explicit length constant, Section <a href="#sec:prescribed-quantitative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prescribed-quantitative">8</a> instead constructs the tail from a common supply of rational numbers whose numerators divide one auxiliary integer. Prime-product residue collisions and the quantitative three-prime theorem provide this supply. A divisor-density property of the unreduced greedy denominator then groups the remaining numerator into few pieces. The resulting length coefficient $257/\log2$ gives the lower slope after padding and absorbing the finitely many small markers.

Throughout, $\mathbb N=\{1,2,\ldots\}$, $\log$ is the natural logarithm, $\log_2 x=(\log x)/(\log 2)$, $\mathrm e(x)=\exp(2\pi i x)$, and sums over real intervals have integer indices. The notations $O(\cdot)$ and $\ll$ have absolute implied constants unless a dependence is indicated. Intermediate unit-fraction lists may contain repetitions. Lemma <a href="#lem:distinct" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:distinct">5</a> removes them when the total is below one; Section <a href="#sec:counting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:counting">6</a> supplies the argument at total one.

<a id="sec:elementary"></a>
### From a dense set to every numerator

The main construction produces short expansions for most numerators over a carefully chosen denominator. We first state exactly what is needed from that construction, and then show why it suffices for every numerator.

<a id="stmt-prop-density"></a>
<div id="prop:density" class="proposition">

**Proposition 4** (A dense set of short expansions). *There are absolute constants $D_M>1$, $L>0$, and $S_0>1$ with the following property. Put $D_X=D_M+2$ and $D_C=4D_X$. For every real $S\ge S_0$ and every integer $C$ satisfying $$e^{D_CS}\le C\le e^{2D_CS},$$ there are an integer $M$ and a set $G\subseteq\{1,\ldots,\lfloor X\rfloor\}$, where $X=e^{D_XS}$, such that $$e^S<M\le e^{D_MS},
 \qquad
 \bigl|\{1,\ldots,\lfloor X\rfloor\}\setminus G\bigr|\le \frac X8.$$ For each $u\in G$, the fraction $u/(MC)$ is a sum of at most $L\log S$ unit fractions with integer denominators at least $2$. Repetitions are allowed.*

</div>

Section <a href="#sec:descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:descent">5</a> proves Proposition <a href="#prop:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:density">4</a>. Its constants and threshold are independent of $C$; the integer $M$ and the set $G$ may depend on $C$. The proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> from this proposition is elementary. We record first how to remove repetitions and how to prepare a denominator in the required range.

<a id="stmt-lem-distinct"></a>
<div id="lem:distinct" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Removing repetitions). *Let $k\ge1$ be an integer. If $0<x<1$ is a sum of $k$ positive unit fractions with integer denominators, then it is a sum of $k$ such fractions with distinct denominators, all at least $2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-distinct"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-distinct</summary>

*Proof.* We use Takenouchi’s argument (Takenouchi 1921, secs. II–III, pp. 79–80) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Takenouchi1921](#ref-Takenouchi1921)]. For fixed $x>0$ and $k$, there are only finitely many nondecreasing lists of $k$ denominators with reciprocal sum $x$. Indeed, the first denominator is at most $k/x$. Once it is chosen, induction applies to the remaining positive sum and the remaining $k-1$ terms; when no terms remain the sum must be zero.

If a denominator occurs twice, apply one of the identities $$\frac2{2p}=\frac1{p+1}+\frac1{p(p+1)},
 \qquad
 \frac2{2p+1}=\frac1{p+1}+\frac1{(p+1)(2p+1)}.$$ Because the total is less than $1$, a denominator $1$, or a repeated denominator $2$, is impossible. Thus $p\ge2$ in the first identity and $p\ge1$ in the second. Each replacement preserves the number of terms and increases the sum of their denominators: the increases are $(p-1)^2$ and $2p^2$, respectively. Finiteness of the set of lists therefore forces termination, at which point all denominators are distinct. Positivity and the unchanged total exclude a denominator $1$ throughout. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-greedy"></a>
<div id="lem:greedy" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Preparing the denominator). *Let $1\le a<b$ be integers and let $T>b$ be real. After at most $$1+\left\lceil\log_2\left(\frac{\log T}{\log 2}\right)\right\rceil$$ greedy steps, either $a/b$ has been expressed as a sum of unit fractions, or its positive remainder can be written as $A/C$, with integers $$1\le A\le a,\qquad T\le C<T^2.$$ All unit denominators produced are at least $2$. The fractions used during this procedure need not be reduced.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-greedy"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-greedy</summary>

*Proof.* For a positive remainder $A/C<1$, set $$z=\left\lceil\frac CA\right\rceil,\qquad
 A'=Az-C,\qquad C'=Cz.$$ Subtracting $1/z$ leaves $A'/C'$. The ceiling inequality gives $0\le A'<A$, while integrality of $A$ gives $z\le C$ and hence $C'\le C^2$. Thus the numerator never exceeds $a$. Stop at zero or at the first positive remainder whose denominator is at least $T$. At such a first crossing, the preceding denominator was less than $T$, so the new one is less than $T^2$.

To bound the number of steps, write $x=A/C$. Since $\lceil1/x\rceil\le 1+1/x$, $$0\le x-\frac1{\lceil1/x\rceil}
 \le \frac{x^2}{1+x}.$$ The first positive remainder is less than $1/2$, and subsequent remainders decrease at least by squaring. After $j\ge1$ positive steps their values therefore satisfy $$x_j\le 2^{-2^{j-1}}.$$ If the procedure has not yet stopped, its positive integer numerator gives $x_j\ge 1/C_j>1/T$. This is impossible when $2^{j-1}\log2\ge\log T$, proving the stated bound. Every current positive value is less than $1$, so $z\ge2$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-greedy-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-greedy</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, assuming Proposition <a href="#prop:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:density">4</a>.* For the upper bound, fix $1\le a<b$, put $S=\log b$, and assume that $S$ is sufficiently large. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:greedy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:greedy">6</a> with $T=e^{D_CS}$. This uses $O(\log S)$ terms. If the procedure terminates, Lemma <a href="#lem:distinct" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:distinct">5</a> gives the required upper bound. Otherwise its remainder $A/C$ satisfies $$1\le A<b,\qquad e^{D_CS}\le C<e^{2D_CS}.$$ Choose $M,G,X$ from Proposition <a href="#prop:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:density">4</a> for this $S,C$. Since $D_X=D_M+2$, $$AM<e^{(D_M+1)S}<X.$$ Let $g=\lfloor X/(AM)\rfloor$ and $n=gAM$. These are positive integers, and the inequality $\lfloor t\rfloor\ge t/2$ for $t\ge1$ gives $$\frac X2\le n\le X.$$ Write $H=\{1,\ldots,\lfloor X\rfloor\}\setminus G$. Among the $n-1$ positive splits $n=u+(n-u)$, at most $2|H|\le X/4$ have an entry outside $G$. Since $n-1\ge X/2-1>X/4$ for large $S$, some split has both entries in $G$. Their expansions together express $$\frac{n}{MC}=\frac{gA}{C}$$ using at most $2L\log S$ unit fractions. Multiplying every denominator by the integer $g$ gives an expansion of $A/C$ of the same length. Adjoin the greedy prefix and apply Lemma <a href="#lem:distinct" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:distinct">5</a> to the total $a/b<1$. The resulting distinct expansion has $O(\log S)$ terms, uniformly in $a$. No reduction of $a/b$, or restriction on the final denominator sizes, has been used.

For the lower bound, consider an expansion of $(b-1)/b$ with $k$ terms and append $1/b$. Sort the resulting $s=k+1$ denominators as $d_1\le\cdots\le d_s$; repetitions are harmless here. Put $L_0=1$ and $L_j=d_1\cdots d_j$. For each $1\le j\le s$, the amount remaining after the first $j-1$ terms is positive and has denominator dividing $L_{j-1}$. Consequently $$\frac1{L_{j-1}}
 \le 1-\sum_{i<j}\frac1{d_i}
 =\sum_{i=j}^s\frac1{d_i}
 \le\frac{s}{d_j}.$$ It follows that $d_j\le sL_{j-1}$ and $L_j\le sL_{j-1}^2$. Induction gives $L_j\le s^{2^j-1}$ and $d_j\le s^{2^{j-1}}$. As $b$ is one of these denominators, $$b\le d_s\le s^{2^{s-1}}=(k+1)^{2^k}.$$ Hence $\log\log b\le k\log2+\log\log(k+1)\le(1+\log2)k$. The numerator $b-1$ therefore supplies the required lower bound for $N(b)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:divisors"></a>
### A uniform divisor moment

We need a divisor estimate for intervals that may be much shorter than their distance from zero. Only divisors up to a specified size are counted: for $X\ge1$ and $n\in\mathbb N$, put $$d_X(n)=\#\{a\in\mathbb N:a\le X,\ a\mid n\}.$$ In the descent, this count bounds how many larger numerators can lead to the same smaller residue. We need to control the combined contribution of residues in a small exceptional set. Hölder’s inequality turns an $r$th-moment bound into a bound proportional to $\delta^{1-1/r}$ for a set occupying a proportion $\delta$ of an interval. Choosing $r$ large limits the cumulative weakening of this bound through the $O(\log S)$ levels of the descent. The moment exponent in the following lemma can be arbitrarily large, provided it is fixed before the parameter $S$ tends to infinity. The proof follows the prime-factor splitting method of Erdős (Erdős 1952, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Erdos1952Divisors](#ref-Erdos1952Divisors)]; the uniform bound for this truncated count is proved in full here.

<a id="stmt-lem-divisor"></a>
<div id="lem:divisor" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:divisor-moment"></a>
**Lemma 7**. *For every fixed pair of real numbers $D,r\ge1$, there is $S_0(D,r)$ such that the following holds for $S\ge S_0(D,r)$. Suppose that $X,Y$ are real numbers and $N$ is a nonnegative integer satisfying $$\frac{S}{2\log S}\le \log X\le DS,\qquad
 X^{1/2}\le Y\le X,\qquad N\le e^{DS}.$$ Then $$\label{eq:divisor-moment}
 \sum_{1\le h\le Y}d_X(N+h)^r\le Y\exp(S^{1/4}).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-divisor"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-divisor</summary>

<a id="eq:divisor-B"></a>
*Proof.* Write $v=\log X$, $E=D+1$, and $$B=1+\log(ES/v).$$ Every integer $n=N+h$ under consideration satisfies $1\le n\le2e^{DS}\le e^{ES}$ for sufficiently large $S$. Moreover, $$\label{eq:divisor-B}
 1<B\le1+\log(2E\log S)=O_D(\log\log S).$$ All estimates below are uniform in $X,Y,N$ in the stated ranges.

<a id="eq:divisor-large-primes"></a>
*Two elementary estimates.* First, consider divisors of $n$ formed from primes at least $z\ge2$. List these prime factors with multiplicity, giving distinct labels to repeated occurrences. There are at most $L=\lfloor ES/\log z\rfloor$ occurrences, and a divisor at most $X$ uses at most $v/\log z$ of them. Counting subsets can only overcount divisors. With $q=v/(ES)\in(0,1)$, their number is therefore at most $$\label{eq:divisor-large-primes}
 \sum_{0\le j\le v/\log z}\binom Lj
 \le q^{-v/\log z}(1+q)^L
 \le \exp\left(\frac{Bv}{\log z}\right).$$ Here the middle inequality follows by inserting the weights $q^j$; the last uses $Lq\le v/\log z$.

<a id="eq:divisor-pointwise"></a>
Split the prime factors at $S^{1/2}$. For each smaller prime there are at most $1+ES/\log2$ possible exponents in a divisor of $n$. Applying <a href="#eq:divisor-large-primes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:divisor-large-primes">[eq:divisor-large-primes]</a> to the remaining primes gives $$\log d_X(n)
 \le S^{1/2}\log(1+ES/\log2)+\frac{2Bv}{\log S}.$$ Since $v\ge S/(2\log S)$, there is a function $\varepsilon_D(S)\to0$, independent of $X,Y,N,h$, such that $$\label{eq:divisor-pointwise}
 d_X(n)^r\le \exp\bigl(r\varepsilon_D(S)v\bigr),
 \qquad
 \varepsilon_D(S)\ll_D
 \frac{(\log S)^2}{S^{1/2}}+\frac{\log\log S}{\log S}.$$

<a id="eq:divisor-local-factor"></a>
<a id="eq:prime-reciprocals"></a>
<a id="eq:divisor-harmonic"></a>
Second, write $\tau(d)$ for the number of positive divisors of $d$. For fixed $r$ and $3/4\le\sigma\le1$, $$\label{eq:divisor-local-factor}
 \log\left(1+\sum_{j\ge1}(j+1)^r p^{-j\sigma}\right)
 \le C_r p^{-\sigma}$$ for every prime $p$. Indeed, the power series $\sum_{j\ge1}(j+1)^r x^{j-1}$ is bounded on $0\le x\le2^{-3/4}<1$. We will also use the elementary estimate $$\label{eq:prime-reciprocals}
 \sum_{p\le T}\frac1p\le e\log(1+\log T)\qquad(T\ge2).$$ To prove it, set $s=1+1/\log T$ and $\zeta(s)=\sum_{a\ge1}a^{-s}$. Euler’s absolutely convergent product and the integral bound $\zeta(s)\le1+1/(s-1)$ give $$\sum_{p\le T}\frac1p
 \le e\sum_p p^{-s}
 \le e\log\zeta(s)
 \le e\log(1+\log T).$$ In particular, multiplicativity and <a href="#eq:divisor-local-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:divisor-local-factor">[eq:divisor-local-factor]</a> imply $$\label{eq:divisor-harmonic}
 \sum_{d\le T}\frac{\tau(d)^r}{d}
 \le \prod_{p\le T}
       \left(1+\sum_{j\ge1}(j+1)^r p^{-j}\right)
 \ll_r(\log(2T))^{C_r}.$$

<a id="eq:divisor-prefix"></a>
<a id="eq:divisor-multiples"></a>
*A prefix of the prime factorization.* For $n>\sqrt Y$, order its prime factors with multiplicity and let $d$ be the longest initial product at most $\sqrt Y$, allowing $d=1$. Let $p$ be the next prime. Then $$d\le\sqrt Y<dp,$$ all prime factors of $d$ are at most $p$, and all those of $n/d$ are at least $p$. The inequality $$d_X(n)\le \tau(d)d_X(n/d)$$ does not require $d$ and $n/d$ to be coprime: a divisor $a\le X$ of $n$ determines $$b=\gcd(a,d),\qquad c=a/b.$$ Then $b\mid d$, $c\mid n/d$, $c\le X$, and the pair $(b,c)$ determines $a$. Thus <a href="#eq:divisor-large-primes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:divisor-large-primes">[eq:divisor-large-primes]</a> gives $$\label{eq:divisor-prefix}
 d_X(n)^r\le\tau(d)^r
          \exp\left(\frac{rBv}{\log p}\right).$$ For any fixed $d\le\sqrt Y$, the number of its multiples in $(N,N+Y]$ is at most $$\label{eq:divisor-multiples}
 \frac Yd+1\le\frac{2Y}{d}.$$ We now sum according to the size of the next prime $p$. If $p$ is large, the remaining factor $n/d$ has few small divisors. If $p$ is smaller, the condition $dp>\sqrt Y$ forces $d$ to be a large product of small primes. The total reciprocal weight of such prefixes is small, so <a href="#eq:divisor-multiples" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:divisor-multiples">[eq:divisor-multiples]</a> limits their occurrence in every shifted interval.

<a id="eq:divisor-large-contribution"></a>
If $\log p\ge v^{15/16}$, the exponential factor in <a href="#eq:divisor-prefix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:divisor-prefix">[eq:divisor-prefix]</a> is at most $e^{rBv^{1/16}}$. For $n\le\sqrt Y$, instead take $d=n$ and use $d_X(n)\le\tau(d)$. By <a href="#eq:divisor-multiples" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:divisor-multiples">[eq:divisor-multiples]</a> and <a href="#eq:divisor-harmonic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:divisor-harmonic">[eq:divisor-harmonic]</a>, these two cases together contribute at most $$\begin{aligned}
 2Ye^{rBv^{1/16}}\sum_{d\le\sqrt Y}\frac{\tau(d)^r}{d}
 &\le Y\exp\!\left(
      O_{D,r}(S^{1/16}\log\log S+\log S)\right).
 \label{eq:divisor-large-contribution}
\end{aligned}$$

<a id="eq:divisor-prefix-lower"></a>
For the remaining integers, $\log p<v^{15/16}$, so $$\label{eq:divisor-prefix-lower}
 \log d>\tfrac12\log Y-\log p
 \ge v/4-v^{15/16}>v/5$$ once $S$ is sufficiently large. This large prefix makes integers with small next prime rare. We estimate the ensuing smooth-prefix sums by Rankin’s exponential weighting method; see (Hildebrand and Tenenbaum 1993, 414, (1.3)) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[HildebrandTenenbaum1993](#ref-HildebrandTenenbaum1993)].

<a id="eq:divisor-small-contribution"></a>
If $p<S^4$, every prime factor of $d$ is smaller than $S^4$. Multiplying each summand by $(d/e^{v/5})^{1/10}>1$ gives $$\sum_{\substack{d>e^{v/5}\\
             \ell\mid d,\ \ell\ {\rm prime}\Rightarrow\ell<S^4}}
       \frac1d
 \le e^{-v/50}
       \prod_{\substack{\ell<S^4\\\ell\ {\rm prime}}}
                      (1-\ell^{-9/10})^{-1}.$$ The logarithm of this product is $O(S^{2/5})$, since $$\sum_{\substack{\ell<S^4\\\ell\ {\rm prime}}}\ell^{-9/10}
 \le\sum_{2\le a<S^4}a^{-9/10}=O(S^{2/5}).$$ Counting multiples by <a href="#eq:divisor-multiples" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:divisor-multiples">[eq:divisor-multiples]</a> and bounding the entire summand by <a href="#eq:divisor-pointwise" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:divisor-pointwise">[eq:divisor-pointwise]</a>, we obtain a contribution at most $$\label{eq:divisor-small-contribution}
 2Y\exp\left(-v/50+O(S^{2/5})+
                         r\varepsilon_D(S)v\right)
 \le 2Ye^{-v/100}.$$ Here $S^{2/5}=o(v)$, uniformly in the allowed range.

<a id="eq:divisor-band"></a>
*The intermediate primes.* It remains to treat $4\log S\le\log p<v^{15/16}$. Partition this range into intervals $[t,2t)$, where $t=2^j4\log S\le v^{15/16}$. There are $O_D(\log S)$ such intervals; the last may extend beyond the upper endpoint. For one interval put $$Q=\frac vt,\qquad \lambda=\frac{\log Q}{10t}.$$ Then $Q\ge v^{1/16}$, and $$0<\lambda
 \le\frac{\log(DS/(4\log S))}{40\log S}<\frac14$$ for sufficiently large $S$. Equations <a href="#eq:divisor-prefix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:divisor-prefix">[eq:divisor-prefix]</a>, <a href="#eq:divisor-multiples" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:divisor-multiples">[eq:divisor-multiples]</a>, and <a href="#eq:divisor-prefix-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:divisor-prefix-lower">[eq:divisor-prefix-lower]</a> bound the contribution of this interval by $$2Ye^{rBQ}
 \sum_{\substack{d>e^{v/5}\\
           \ell\mid d,\ \ell\ {\rm prime}\Rightarrow\ell\le e^{2t}}}
       \frac{\tau(d)^r}{d}.$$ The last sum is at most $$e^{-\lambda v/5}
 \prod_{\substack{\ell\le e^{2t}\\\ell\ {\rm prime}}}
 \left(1+\sum_{j\ge1}(j+1)^r\ell^{-j(1-\lambda)}\right).$$ By <a href="#eq:divisor-local-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:divisor-local-factor">[eq:divisor-local-factor]</a> and <a href="#eq:prime-reciprocals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prime-reciprocals">[eq:prime-reciprocals]</a>, the logarithm of this product is bounded by $$C_r\sum_{\substack{\ell\le e^{2t}\\\ell\ {\rm prime}}}
                   \ell^{-1+\lambda}
 \ll_r e^{2t\lambda}\log(2t)
 =Q^{1/5}O_r(\log(2t)).$$ The contribution of the interval is consequently at most $$\label{eq:divisor-band}
 2Y\exp\left(rBQ-\frac{Q\log Q}{50}
                         +O_r(Q^{1/5}\log(2t))\right).$$ Both positive terms in this exponent are uniformly negligible compared with $Q\log Q$. Indeed, <a href="#eq:divisor-B" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:divisor-B">[eq:divisor-B]</a> and $Q\ge v^{1/16}$ give $$\frac{rB}{\log Q}
 \ll_{D,r}\frac{\log\log S}{\log S}\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad
 \frac{Q^{1/5}\log(2t)}{Q\log Q}
 \ll v^{-1/20}\longrightarrow0.$$ Thus <a href="#eq:divisor-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:divisor-band">[eq:divisor-band]</a> is at most $2Y\exp(-Q\log Q/100)\le2Y$, after increasing $S_0(D,r)$. Summing the intervals costs $O_D(Y\log S)$.

Combining this bound with <a href="#eq:divisor-large-contribution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:divisor-large-contribution">[eq:divisor-large-contribution]</a> and <a href="#eq:divisor-small-contribution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:divisor-small-contribution">[eq:divisor-small-contribution]</a> gives $$\sum_{1\le h\le Y}d_X(N+h)^r
 \le Y\exp\!\left(
         O_{D,r}(S^{1/16}\log\log S+\log S)\right)
       +2Ye^{-v/100}+O_D(Y\log S).$$ The logarithmic loss is $o(S^{1/4})$, proving <a href="#eq:divisor-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:divisor-moment">[eq:divisor-moment]</a>. Every enlargement of the threshold depended only on the fixed $D,r$; this completes the required uniformity check. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:residues"></a>
### Divisors with small residues

We now construct the auxiliary denominator. Its useful divisors make most residues small at each of a sequence of scales. At the smallest scales, several independent lists of divisors ensure that every numerator has a suitable choice.

The underlying identity is elementary. For positive integers $M,Q,u,t$ with $t\mid M$, set $$z=\left\lceil\frac{Qt}{u}\right\rceil,
 \qquad h=uz-Qt.$$ Then $z\ge1$, $0\le h<u$, and <a id="eq:residue-step"></a>$$\label{eq:residue-step}
 \frac{u}{MQ}=\frac{1}{(M/t)z}+\frac{h}{MQz}.$$ Thus a small least nonnegative residue of $-Qt$ modulo $u$ leaves a small numerator. A zero residue finishes the expansion.

We use this identity with two denominator factors. At high levels we take $Q=C$: the large factor $C$ supplies cancellation in reciprocal phases as $u$ varies. At lower levels we take $Q=1$ and construct expansions of $u/M$. Dividing such an expansion by $C$ gives one of $u/(MC)$, so the two regimes can be joined. The switch below is made when the entire level cutoff is at most $e^S$; then $u/M<1$ because our construction ensures $M>e^S$.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### The denominator and the residue statement

<a id="eq:C-range"></a>
Fix the absolute constants <a id="eq:residue-constants"></a>$$\label{eq:residue-constants}
 \begin{gathered}
 K=100,\quad R=1000,\quad D_0=100000,\quad \eta=10^{-4},\\
 D_M=(2R+2)(K+2),\quad D_X=D_M+2,\quad D_C=4D_X.
 \end{gathered}$$ Here $K$ sets the prime scale, $R$ is the number of independent blocks, $D_0$ sets the binary cutoff, and $\eta$ sets the rate of descent. These numerical choices leave room in the estimates below. Let $S$ tend to infinity, put $m=\lfloor S/\log S\rfloor$, and fix an integer $$\label{eq:C-range}
 e^{D_CS}\le C\le e^{2D_CS}.$$ Define <a id="eq:levels"></a>$$\label{eq:levels}
 \begin{gathered}
 \rho=e^{-\eta m},\qquad X_j=e^{D_XS}\rho^j,\\
 d=\min\{j\ge0:X_j\le e^m\},\qquad
 C_j=
 \begin{cases}
 C,&X_j>e^S,\\
 1,&X_j\le e^S.
 \end{cases}
 \end{gathered}$$ These cutoffs need not be integers. Every set or sum indexed by a cutoff below consists of integers in the indicated range. For $0\le j\le d$, write <a id="eq:least-residue"></a>$$\label{eq:least-residue}
 h_{j,t}(u)=u\left\lceil\frac{C_jt}{u}\right\rceil-C_jt.$$ In particular, $h_{j,t}(u)$ is the least nonnegative residue of $-C_jt$ modulo $u$.

<a id="stmt-lem-residues"></a>
<div id="lem:residues" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8**. *For all sufficiently large $S$ and every integer $C$ satisfying <a href="#eq:C-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:C-range">[eq:C-range]</a>, there are an integer $M$ and indexed lists $T_0,T_1,\ldots,T_R$, each with $2^m$ entries, such that:*

1.  *$e^S<M\le e^{D_MS}$, and the least power of $2$ at least $S^{D_0}$ divides $M$. Every entry of every $T_i$ divides $M$.*

2.  *For each $0\le j<d$, let $T=T_0$ if $X_j>e^S$, and let $T=T_1$ otherwise. Apart from at most $X_j e^{-c_*m}$ integers $u\in(X_{j+1},X_j]$, at least $\rho |T|/2$ entries $t\in T$ satisfy $$h_{j,t}(u)\le X_{j+1},
     \qquad c_*=0.001.$$*

3.  *For every integer $S^{D_0}<u\le e^m$, some entry $t$ of one of $T_1,\ldots,T_R$ satisfies $$u\lceil t/u\rceil-t\le u^{1-\eta}.$$*

*Repeated values in a list are counted with their indices. The threshold on $S$ is independent of $C$.*

</div>

Here is the construction used to prove the lemma. Let $\mathcal P$ be the set of primes in $[S^K,2S^K]$, and put $P=|\mathcal P|$. The prime number theorem gives $P\ge S^{K-1}$ for sufficiently large $S$; see, for example, (Selberg 1949, 305, (1.1)) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Selberg1949](#ref-Selberg1949)]. Choose distinct $p_1,\ldots,p_m\in\mathcal P$, and let $$T_0=\left(\prod_{j=1}^m p_j^{I_j}\right)_{I\in\{0,1\}^m}.$$ Its entries are distinct. For each $1\le i\le R$, choose $2m$ independent uniform samples $p_{i,j,\epsilon}\in\mathcal P$, where $1\le j\le m$ and $\epsilon\in\{0,1\}$; all samples in different blocks are also independent. Set $$\begin{gathered}
 T_i=\left(\prod_{j=1}^m p_{i,j,I_j}\right)_{I\in\{0,1\}^m},\\
 M=2^a\prod_{j=1}^m p_j
       \prod_{i=1}^R\prod_{j=1}^m p_{i,j,0}p_{i,j,1},
 \end{gathered}$$ where $2^a$ is the least power of $2$ at least $S^{D_0}$. Sampling is with replacement. Even when primes repeat, each list entry uses a submultiset of the factors of $M$, so it divides $M$.

<a id="eq:product-size"></a>
The size bounds hold for every realization: $$\log M\ge Km\log S\ge K(S-\log S)>S,$$ whereas $$\log M
 \le D_0\log S+\log2+(2R+1)m(K\log S+\log2)
 \le D_MS$$ eventually. The last inequality follows because $(2R+1)K<D_M$. Every entry of every list also satisfies $$\label{eq:product-size}
 1\le t\le e^{(K+1)S}.$$

We record the endpoint facts used below and in the descent. For sufficiently large $S$, <a id="eq:level-endpoints"></a>$$\label{eq:level-endpoints}
 \begin{gathered}
 \frac{S}{2\log S}\le m\le\frac S{\log S},\\
 d=\left\lceil\frac{D_XS-m}{\eta m}\right\rceil
       \le K_d\log S,\qquad K_d=\frac{3D_X}{\eta},\\
 e^{(1-\eta)m}<X_d\le e^m,\qquad
 X_{j+1}\ge\sqrt{X_j}\quad(0\le j<d).
 \end{gathered}$$ Indeed, for $j<d$, $\log X_j>m$, so $\log X_{j+1}=\log X_j-\eta m\ge(1-\eta)\log X_j$. Moreover $C_d=1$, and $C_{j+1}\mid C_j$.

Table <a href="#tab:regimes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:regimes">1</a> summarizes the roles of the lists. The upper two rows are classified by the level cutoff $X_j$, so a high level may include some numerators below $e^S$. The binary step is carried out in Section <a href="#sec:descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:descent">5</a>.

<div id="tab:regimes">

| Range and denominator factor                   | Divisors used                  | Coverage                      |
|:-----------------------------------------------|:-------------------------------|:------------------------------|
| High levels: $X_j>e^S$, $C_j=C$                | Deterministic list $T_0$       | Most numerators at each level |
| Middle levels: $e^m<X_j\le e^S$, $C_j=1$       | The same random list $T_1$     | Most numerators at each level |
| Terminal range: $S^{D_0}<u\le e^m$, factor $1$ | Some list $T_i$, $1\le i\le R$ | Every numerator               |
| Binary range: $1\le u\le S^{D_0}$, factor $1$  | Powers of $2$ dividing $M$     | Every numerator               |

The four stages of the numerator descent. The precise residue guarantees are stated in Lemma <a href="#lem:residues" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:residues">8</a>.

</div>

It remains to select the samples so that the two residue assertions hold. We first turn Fourier bounds into small residues, then prove the needed bound for $T_0$. Lemma <a href="#lem:random" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:random">12</a> treats the random lists, and Section <a href="#subsec:selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="subsec:selection">4.4</a> makes the common choice.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Discrepancy and the deterministic list

<a id="eq:erdos-turan"></a>
We use the Erdős–Turán discrepancy inequality (Erdős and Turán 1948, pt. I, Theorem III) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[ErdosTuran1948](#ref-ErdosTuran1948)]. For any nonempty finite indexed list $(x_t)_{t\in T}$ of real numbers, any interval $J\subset[0,1)$, and any integer $H\ge1$, it states that $$\label{eq:erdos-turan}
 \left|
 \frac{\#\{t:\{x_t\}\in J\}}{|T|}-|J|
 \right|
 \ll \frac1H+
 \sum_{\ell=1}^H\frac1\ell
 \left|\frac1{|T|}\sum_{t\in T}\mathrm e(\ell x_t)\right|.$$ The implied constant is absolute, and list multiplicities are retained. The interval convention used below also retains entries at zero. For a finite list, a sufficiently small common positive rotation sends membership in $[0,\delta)$ to membership in $(0,\delta]$, including all repeated endpoint entries correctly, while preserving every Fourier modulus. Thus the interval convention in the original proof (Erdős and Turán 1948, pt. II, Sections 11–16) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[ErdosTuran1948](#ref-ErdosTuran1948)] gives the form needed here.

<a id="stmt-lem-discrepancy"></a>
<div id="lem:discrepancy" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9**. *For all sufficiently large real $w$, suppose $u,Q$ are positive integers and $T$ is a nonempty finite list of positive integers. If $$\left|\frac1{|T|}\sum_{t\in T}\mathrm e(\ell Qt/u)\right|
 \le e^{-3\eta w}
 \quad
 \left(1\le\ell\le\lfloor e^{4\eta w}\rfloor\right),$$ then at least $e^{-\eta w}|T|/2$ entries satisfy $$0\le u\lceil Qt/u\rceil-Qt<e^{-\eta w}u.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-discrepancy"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-discrepancy</summary>

*Proof.* Apply <a href="#eq:erdos-turan" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:erdos-turan">[eq:erdos-turan]</a> to $x_t=-Qt/u$ and $J=[0,e^{-\eta w})$. Conjugation leaves the Fourier bounds unchanged. The discrepancy is $$O\bigl(e^{-4\eta w}+(1+4\eta w)e^{-3\eta w}\bigr)
 =o(e^{-\eta w}).$$ It is therefore at most $e^{-\eta w}/2$ eventually. Finally, $\{-Qt/u\}=(u\lceil Qt/u\rceil-Qt)/u$, including when the residue is zero. ◻

</details>

At a level $X_j>e^S$, the large factor $C$ makes the reciprocal phases oscillate as $u$ varies. The following elementary estimate will make that observation uniform.

<a id="stmt-lem-reciprocal-phase"></a>
<div id="lem:reciprocal-phase" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10**. *Fix $B\ge4$. There are constants $A_B,\delta_B>0$ such that, for every sufficiently large $U$, every real $Z$ with $U^4\le |Z|\le U^B$, and every interval $I\subset[U,2U]$, $$\left|\sum_{n\in I}\mathrm e(Z/n)\right|\le A_BU^{1-\delta_B}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-reciprocal-phase"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-reciprocal-phase</summary>

*Proof.* We give the derivative argument, including the elementary estimates it uses. This is the classical differencing method of van der Corput; see (Graham and Kolesnik 1991, Lemma 2.5) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[GrahamKolesnik1991](#ref-GrahamKolesnik1991)] for differencing and (Graham and Kolesnik 1991, Theorem 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[GrahamKolesnik1991](#ref-GrahamKolesnik1991)] for the second-derivative estimate.

<a id="eq:differencing"></a>
First, if $|a_n|\le1$ is supported on an interval of length at most $U$, then for $2\le L\le U$, $$\label{eq:differencing}
 U^{-2}\left|\sum_n a_n\right|^2
 \ll L^{-1}
 +\max_{1\le h<L}U^{-1}
        \left|\sum_n a_{n+h}\overline{a_n}\right|.$$ To see this, express the sum as $L^{-1}\sum_n\sum_{h=1}^L a_{n+h}$, apply Cauchy–Schwarz in $n$, and expand the square. The outer support has length at most $U+L+2$, the diagonal contributes $O(LU)$, and the off-diagonal terms give <a href="#eq:differencing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:differencing">[eq:differencing]</a>.

<a id="eq:second-derivative"></a>
We also need the second derivative bound $$\label{eq:second-derivative}
 \left|\sum_{n\in J}\mathrm e(g(n))\right|
 \ll_A U\sqrt\lambda+\lambda^{-1/2}+1
 \quad\text{if}\quad
 \lambda\le |g''(x)|\le A\lambda$$ on an interval $J$ of length at most $U$, for $g\in C^2(J)$. Here is an elementary proof. For $\lambda$ above a fixed positive constant the trivial bound suffices. Otherwise put $\beta=\sqrt\lambda<1/4$. The derivative $g'$ is monotone and traverses a range of length $O_A(U\lambda)$. The portions where $g'$ is within $\beta$ of an integer have $O_A(U\lambda+1)$ components and total length $O_A(U\beta+\beta/\lambda)$, since $|g''|\ge\lambda$. Their integer points contribute the same bound after adding the number of components.

On each remaining interval $g'$ is monotone and stays between $q+\beta$ and $q+1-\beta$ for some integer $q$. The corresponding sum is $O(\beta^{-1})$. Indeed the increments $g(n+1)-g(n)$ are monotone in that same interval, and summation by parts in $$\mathrm e(g(n))=
 \frac{\mathrm e(g(n+1))-\mathrm e(g(n))}
      {\mathrm e(g(n+1)-g(n))-1}$$ gives this bound: the reciprocal denominator has supremum and total variation $O(\beta^{-1})$. Boundary terms add at most a constant per interval. Summing over the $O_A(U\lambda+1)$ intervals proves <a href="#eq:second-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:second-derivative">[eq:second-derivative]</a>.

<a id="eq:derivative-order"></a>
Now let $k$ be a nearest integer to $\log|Z|/\log U$, and set $$Q_B=\lceil B\rceil+1,\qquad
 r=k-2,\qquad L=\lfloor U^{1/(10k)}\rfloor.$$ There are only finitely many possible orders: $$\label{eq:derivative-order}
 4\le k\le Q_B,\qquad
 U^{-3/2}\le |Z|U^{-k-1}\le U^{-1/2}.$$ Apply <a href="#eq:differencing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:differencing">[eq:differencing]</a> $r$ times to $a_n=\mathbf1_I(n)\mathrm e(f(n))$, where $f(x)=Z/x$, extending the sequence by zero outside $I$. For positive shifts $h_1,\ldots,h_r<L$, the last phase is $$g(x)=\Delta_{h_1}\cdots\Delta_{h_r}f(x),
 \qquad \Delta_hf(x)=f(x+h)-f(x).$$ Its support is the interval on which both $x$ and $x+h_1+\cdots+h_r$ belong to $I$. All intermediate points $x+t_1+\cdots+t_r$, $0\le t_i\le h_i$, therefore remain in $[U,2U]$. Empty supports contribute zero.

The fundamental theorem of calculus gives $$g''(x)=
 \int_0^{h_1}\!\cdots\!\int_0^{h_r}
 f^{(k)}(x+t_1+\cdots+t_r)\,dt_r\cdots dt_1.$$ Since $f^{(k)}(x)=(-1)^k k!Zx^{-k-1}$ has constant sign, $$\frac{k!}{2^{k+1}}\Lambda
 \le |g''(x)|\le k!\Lambda,
 \qquad \Lambda=|Z|U^{-k-1}\prod_{i=1}^r h_i.$$ By <a href="#eq:derivative-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:derivative-order">[eq:derivative-order]</a> and the choice of $L$, $$U^{-3/2}\le\Lambda
 \le U^{-1/2+(k-2)/(10k)}\le U^{-2/5}.$$ Thus <a href="#eq:second-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:second-derivative">[eq:second-derivative]</a> bounds each last correlation by $O_k(U^{4/5}+U^{3/4}+1)=O_k(U^{4/5})$.

For clarity, normalize every correlation by $U$, and let $\sigma_j$ be their maximum after $j$ shifts. Then $$\sigma_{k-2}\ll_k U^{-1/5},
 \qquad \sigma_j^2\ll L^{-1}+\sigma_{j+1}.$$ Induction, using $L\ge U^{1/(10k)}/2$ for large $U$, yields $$\sigma_0\ll_k U^{-1/(10k\,2^{k-2})}.$$ Taking the largest implied constant over $4\le k\le Q_B$ and, for example, $\delta_B=(10Q_B2^{Q_B})^{-1}$ proves the lemma. In particular, although $k$ depends on $Z,U$, the final constants depend only on $B$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-deterministic"></a>
<div id="lem:deterministic" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:deterministic-mean"></a>
**Lemma 11**. *For the deterministic list $T_0$ constructed above, every level $0\le j<d$ with $X=X_j>e^S$, and every integer $1\le\ell\le H:=\lfloor e^{4\eta m}\rfloor$, one has $$\label{eq:deterministic-mean}
 \frac1X\sum_{X_{j+1}<u\le X}
 \left|\frac1{|T_0|}\sum_{t\in T_0}\mathrm e(\ell Ct/u)\right|^2
 \le e^{-0.01m}$$ for all sufficiently large $S$, uniformly in $C$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-deterministic"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-deterministic</summary>

*Proof.* Write $Y=X_{j+1}=\rho X$; then $Y>e^{S-\eta m}\ge e^{0.9S}$. Expanding the square, the diagonal contributes at most $2^{-m}$ because the $2^m$ subset products are distinct. For an off-diagonal pair put $Z=\ell C(t-t')$. The difference $t-t'$ is a nonzero integer, and <a href="#eq:C-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:C-range">[eq:C-range]</a> and <a href="#eq:product-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:product-size">[eq:product-size]</a> give $$e^{D_CS}\le |Z|\le e^{(2D_C+K+2)S}.$$ Split $(Y,X]$ into intersections with dyadic intervals $[U,2U]$. We may take $Y/2\le U\le X$, so for large $S$, $$e^{0.8S}\le U\le e^{D_XS},\qquad
 U^4\le |Z|\le U^{3D_C}.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:reciprocal-phase" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:reciprocal-phase">10</a>, with the fixed value $B=3D_C$, bounds the sum over each piece by $O(Ue^{-0.8\delta_BS})$. The sum of the dyadic $U$’s is $O(X)$. Thus $$\left|\sum_{Y<u\le X}\mathrm e(Z/u)\right|\ll Xe^{-cS}$$ for an absolute $c>0$. Averaging over all off-diagonal pairs cancels their number against $|T_0|^2$. The left side of <a href="#eq:deterministic-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:deterministic-mean">[eq:deterministic-mean]</a> is consequently at most $$2^{-m}+O(e^{-cS})\le e^{-0.01m}$$ eventually, since $S/m\to\infty$. ◻

</details>

The deterministic estimate controls the high levels for every permitted $C$. We next prove the random estimate for the lower levels and for individual terminal numerators.

<a id="subsec:random"></a>
#### Random products

For the smaller moduli, two independent products of sampled primes supply the cancellation. Their integer values are smaller than a suitable divisor of the modulus, so unique factorization controls their residue distributions.

<a id="stmt-lem-random"></a>
<div id="lem:random" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12**. *Let $K=100$, $D_0=100000$, and $m=\lfloor S/\log S\rfloor$. Let $\mathcal P$ be a set of primes in $[S^K,2S^K]$ with $P=|\mathcal P|\ge S^{K-1}$, and sample $$p_{j,\epsilon}\qquad
 (1\le j\le m,\ \epsilon\in\{0,1\})$$ independently and uniformly from $\mathcal P$. For $I\in\{0,1\}^m$, put $t_I=\prod_{j=1}^m p_{j,I_j}$. For every integer $u$ such that $$D_0\log S\le V:=\log u\le S,
 \qquad w=\min(m,V),$$ and every integer $1\le l\le \exp(0.005w)$, one has <a id="eq:random-second-moment"></a>$$\label{eq:random-second-moment}
 \mathbb E\left|2^{-m}\sum_{I\in\{0,1\}^m}\mathrm e(lt_I/u)\right|^2
 \le \exp(-0.01w)$$ for all sufficiently large $S$. The onset is absolute and uniform in $u,l,\mathcal P$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-random"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-random</summary>

*Proof.* Expanding the square gives $$4^{-m}\sum_{I,J\in\{0,1\}^m}
       \mathbb E\,\mathrm e\bigl(l(t_I-t_J)/u\bigr).$$ For uniformly counted pairs $I,J$, their Hamming distance $B$ has distribution $\operatorname{Bin}(m,1/2)$. The exponential Markov inequality gives $$\mathbb P(B<m/4)
 \le 2^{m/4}\mathbb E\,2^{-B}
 =2^{m/4}(3/4)^m
 \le e^{-m/16}.$$ We bound these pairs trivially. Fix any remaining pair, and set $$s=\left\lfloor\frac{0.75V}{K\log S}\right\rfloor.$$ Since $V/(K\log S)\ge1000$, and $V\le S$, we have <a id="eq:random-product-lengths"></a>$$\label{eq:random-product-lengths}
 \frac{0.74V}{K\log S}\le s\le\frac{0.75V}{K\log S},
 \qquad 2s\le m/4$$ for sufficiently large $S$.

Select the first $2s$ differing coordinates in increasing order, and divide them into two sets of $s$ coordinates each. This choice depends only on $I,J$, not on the sampled primes. Expose every labelled prime sample except the $I$-selected sample at these $2s$ coordinates. Then $t_J$ is fixed and $$t_I=U_0U_1U_2,$$ where $U_0$ is fixed by the exposure and $U_1,U_2$ are independent products of $s$ fresh uniform samples each. Equal prime values cause no difficulty: the $I$-selected and $J$-selected variables at a differing coordinate are distinct independent samples.

Write $$g=\gcd(lU_0,u),\qquad q=u/g.$$ The coefficient $lU_0/u$ reduces to a fraction with denominator $q$. We will show that $q$ is usually large enough for distinct integer values of each fresh product to remain distinct modulo $q$. Their small point probabilities will then give cancellation by additive-character orthogonality.

<a id="eq:random-gcd"></a>
We first bound the probability, over the exposed variables, that $q<u^{0.9}$. Since $l\le u^{0.005}$ and $\gcd(lU_0,u)\le l\gcd(U_0,u)$, this event implies $\gcd(U_0,u)>u^{0.095}$. Call a sample contributing to $U_0$ a hit if its prime value divides $u$, and count hits with their sample multiplicities. If their number is $B_0$, then $$\gcd(U_0,u)\le \prod_{\text{hit samples }p}p
       \le S^{(K+1)B_0}.$$ Thus failure requires at least $$k_0=\left\lceil\frac{0.08V}{(K+1)\log S}\right\rceil$$ hits. At most $S$ primes in the sampling interval divide $u$, so each sample has hit probability at most $S/P\le S^{-(K-2)}$. There are at most $m\le S$ independent samples in $U_0$. A union bound over sets of $k_0$ positions yields $$\label{eq:random-gcd}
 \mathbb P(q<u^{0.9})
 \le S^{-(K-3)k_0}
 \le \exp\left(-\frac{0.08(K-3)}{K+1}V\right)
 \le u^{-0.05}.$$ If $k_0>m-2s$, the event is empty. This argument allows prime powers in $u$ and repeated sampled primes.

Now fix an exposure with $q\ge u^{0.9}$. Every possible value of either fresh product is at most $$(2S^K)^s\le S^{(K+1)s}\le u^{0.7575}<u^{0.8}<q.$$ By unique factorization, an integer can arise as the product of at most $s!$ ordered $s$-tuples of primes. Consequently each atom in the distribution of $U_i$, for $i=1,2$, has probability at most $$\frac{s!}{P^s}\le S^{-(K-2)s}
      \le u^{-0.7252}\le u^{-0.7}.$$ Reduction modulo $q$ is injective on these integer values. The probability vectors $\alpha,\beta$ of $U_1,U_2$ on $\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z$ therefore satisfy $$\|\alpha\|_2,\|\beta\|_2\le u^{-0.35}.$$

The reduced fraction $lU_0/u=c/q$ has $\gcd(c,q)=1$. Complete additive-character orthogonality gives $$\sum_{x\bmod q}\left|\sum_{y\bmod q}\beta_y\mathrm e(cxy/q)\right|^2
       =q\sum_{y\bmod q}|\beta_y|^2.$$ Indeed the inner sum over $x$ vanishes unless $q\mid c(y-y')$, equivalently $y=y'$ modulo $q$. This identity holds for composite $q$ as well. Cauchy–Schwarz gives $$\left|\sum_{x,y\bmod q}\alpha_x\beta_y\mathrm e(cxy/q)\right|
 \le \sqrt q\,\|\alpha\|_2\|\beta\|_2
 \le u^{-0.2}.$$ The factor $\mathrm e(-lt_J/u)$ is fixed under the exposure. Averaging this conditional estimate and using <a href="#eq:random-gcd" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:random-gcd">[eq:random-gcd]</a> bounds the contribution of our fixed pair $I,J$ in absolute value by $u^{-0.05}+u^{-0.2}$.

Combining this with the Hamming exception, we obtain $$\mathbb E\left|2^{-m}\sum_I\mathrm e(lt_I/u)\right|^2
 \le e^{-m/16}+u^{-0.05}+u^{-0.2}
 \le 3e^{-0.05w}
 \le e^{-0.01w}.$$ The final inequality is uniform because $\min(m,D_0\log S)$ tends to infinity with $S$. ◻

</details>

<a id="subsec:selection"></a>
#### A simultaneous choice of divisors

We now choose all random blocks at once. One block will suffice at all intermediate levels outside small exceptional sets; the independent blocks together will cover every terminal integer.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-random-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-random</summary>

*Proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:residues" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:residues">8</a>.* The construction already gives the required size of $M$, its power of two, and divisibility of every list entry, for every realization of the samples. We prove the two residue properties.

Put $H=\lfloor e^{4\eta m}\rfloor$. At a level with $X_j>e^S$, Lemma <a href="#lem:deterministic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:deterministic">11</a> and Markov’s inequality show that the number of integers $u\in(X_{j+1},X_j]$ for which $$\left|2^{-m}\sum_{t\in T_0}\mathrm e(lCt/u)\right|>e^{-3\eta m}$$ at some $1\le l\le H$ is at most $$X_j H e^{6\eta m}e^{-0.01m}
 \le X_j e^{-0.009m}.$$ Conjugation changes the Fourier sign without changing the absolute value. Lemma <a href="#lem:discrepancy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:discrepancy">9</a>, with scale $m$, therefore gives at least $\rho|T_0|/2$ entries with $$h_{j,t}(u)<\rho u\le X_{j+1}$$ outside that exceptional set. This holds separately at every such level and requires no probabilistic selection.

Next suppose $j<d$ and $X_j\le e^S$. Then $X_j>e^m$, and every $u\in(X_{j+1},X_j]$ satisfies $$(1-\eta)m<\log u\le S,\qquad
 w=\min(m,\log u)\ge(1-\eta)m.$$ For sufficiently large $S$, these integers satisfy the lower bound in Lemma <a href="#lem:random" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:random">12</a>. Moreover $4\eta m\le0.005w$, so that Lemma applies to all $1\le l\le H$. In block $1$, Markov’s inequality followed by the frequency union bound shows that $$\begin{aligned}
 &\mathbb P\left(
   \max_{1\le l\le H}
   \left|2^{-m}\sum_{t\in T_1}\mathrm e(lt/u)\right|>e^{-3\eta m}
 \right)\\
 &\hspace{2em}\le
 \exp\bigl((10\eta-0.01(1-\eta))m\bigr)
 =e^{-0.008999m}\le e^{-0.005m}.
\end{aligned}$$ Let $B_j$ count integers in this level that fail this Fourier bound. Linearity of expectation and another application of Markov give $$\mathbb EB_j\le X_j e^{-0.005m},
 \qquad
 \mathbb P(B_j>X_j e^{-0.001m})\le e^{-0.004m}.$$ As $d=O(\log S)$, with probability $1-o(1)$ these bounds hold at every level under consideration. No independence between different integers or levels is needed. Here $C_j=1$, so Lemma <a href="#lem:discrepancy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:discrepancy">9</a> again gives the required $\rho|T_1|/2$ entries outside the exceptional sets.

Finally fix an integer $S^{D_0}<u\le e^m$. Now $w=\log u$, and Lemma <a href="#lem:random" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:random">12</a>, Markov’s inequality and the union over $1\le l\le\lfloor u^{4\eta}\rfloor$ give $$\begin{aligned}
 &
 \mathbb P\left(
  \max_{1\le l\le\lfloor u^{4\eta}\rfloor}
   \left|2^{-m}\sum_{t\in T_i}\mathrm e(lt/u)\right|>u^{-3\eta}
 \right)\\
 &\hspace{2em}\le u^{10\eta-0.01}
 =u^{-0.009}\le u^{-0.005}
\end{aligned}$$ in each block $i$. On success, Lemma <a href="#lem:discrepancy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:discrepancy">9</a>, with scale $\log u$, supplies a list entry for which $$u\lceil t/u\rceil-t<u^{1-\eta}.$$ The $R=1000$ blocks are independent for this fixed $u$. The probability that every block fails is therefore at most $u^{-5}$. A union bound over all terminal integers shows that $$\mathbb P(\text{some terminal integer has no successful block})
 \le \sum_{u>S^{D_0}}u^{-5}=o(1).$$ Thus every terminal integer has a suitable divisor, simultaneously, with probability tending to one.

The sum of this failure probability and the middle-level failure probability is $o(1)$. Hence a realization satisfying both sets of requirements exists. Fix it and the resulting $M$. Since $e^{-0.009m}\le e^{-0.001m}$, the same $c_*=0.001$ is valid at every level. This proves both properties for one common $M$, for the fixed $S$ and $C$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:descent"></a>
### Propagating the exceptional sets

We now prove Proposition <a href="#prop:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:density">4</a>. Lemma <a href="#lem:residues" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:residues">8</a> provides many choices for making the numerator smaller. Some of those choices may land at numerators whose expansions are still unknown. The divisor moment in Lemma <a href="#lem:divisor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:divisor">7</a> bounds how often this can happen. An individual residue may have many possible predecessors; the moment estimate controls their combined contribution over a small exceptional set of residues.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-random-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-random</summary>

<a id="eq:descent-depth"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:density">4</a>.* Use the absolute constants and levels of Lemma <a href="#lem:residues" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:residues">8</a>. Before choosing $S$, fix $$K_d=\frac{3D_X}{\eta},\qquad
 D_*=2D_C+D_M+1,
 \qquad r\in\mathbb N,\quad r\ge \max\{2,8K_d\},
 \qquad \alpha=1-\frac1r.$$ All subsequent lower bounds on $S$ may depend on these fixed constants. In particular, the moment order $r$ is independent of $S$. For sufficiently large $S$, $$\label{eq:descent-depth}
 m\ge\frac{S}{2\log S},
 \qquad
 d=\left\lceil\frac{D_XS-m}{\eta m}\right\rceil
 \le \frac{2D_X}{\eta}\log S+1
 \le K_d\log S.$$ Fix an integer $C$ in the range of Proposition <a href="#prop:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:density">4</a>, and choose the common integer $M$ supplied by Lemma <a href="#lem:residues" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:residues">8</a>.

Every fraction $u/(MC_j)$ with $1\le u\le X_j$ is less than one. Indeed, if $C_j=C$, then $u\le X_0=e^{D_XS}<MC$; if $C_j=1$, then $u\le e^S<M$. Moreover $C_d=1$, since $X_d\le e^m<e^S$. We first produce expansions at this last level, and then work backwards.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
###### The last level.

The power of two dividing $M$ is at least $S^{D_0}$. For any integer $1\le u\le S^{D_0}$, write $u$ in binary. Every power $2^k$ that occurs divides $M$, and $$\frac{2^k}{M}=\frac1{M/2^k}.$$ This gives an expansion of $u/M$ with at most $1+D_0\log S/\log 2$ terms.

If $S^{D_0}<u\le e^m$, the last part of Lemma <a href="#lem:residues" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:residues">8</a> gives a divisor $t\mid M$ for which $$z=\left\lceil\frac tu\right\rceil,\qquad
 h=uz-t,\qquad 0\le h\le u^{1-\eta}.$$ The identity <a id="eq:terminal-descent"></a>$$\label{eq:terminal-descent}
 \frac uM=\frac1{(M/t)z}+\frac1z\frac hM$$ adds one unit fraction. If $h=0$, the expansion ends; otherwise an expansion of $h/M$ can be divided by the positive integer $z$. As long as the numerator remains above $S^{D_0}$, its logarithm decreases by a factor at most $1-\eta$ at each step. It starts at most $m\le S$, so after at most $$1+\left\lceil\frac{\log S}{-\log(1-\eta)}\right\rceil$$ steps it has either vanished or entered the binary range. Thus an absolute constant $B_0$ bounds the length of every terminal expansion by $B_0\log S$. All denominators are integers. Since every term is positive and the total is less than one, every denominator is at least two.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
###### Working backwards.

<a id="eq:descent-length"></a>
Set $$G_d=\{1,\ldots,\lfloor X_d\rfloor\}.$$ At a level $j<d$, write the list prescribed by Lemma <a href="#lem:residues" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:residues">8</a> as $(t_{j,i})_{i\in I_j}$: it is the deterministic list when $X_j>e^S$, and the first random list otherwise. Its entries may coincide; their indices are always retained. Having defined $G_{j+1}$, let <a id="eq:good-backwards"></a>$$\label{eq:good-backwards}
 \begin{aligned}
 G_j=G_{j+1}\ \cup\ \bigl\{u\in\mathbb N:\ &1\le u\le X_j,\\
 &h_{j,t_{j,i}}(u)\in G_{j+1}\cup\{0\}
       \text{ for some }i\in I_j\bigr\}.
 \end{aligned}$$ These sets have the asserted expansions. For membership inherited directly from $G_{j+1}$, divide the known expansion by the integer $C_j/C_{j+1}$. For membership obtained from a residue, put $$z=\left\lceil\frac{C_jt_{j,i}}u\right\rceil,\qquad
 h=uz-C_jt_{j,i}.$$ Then <a id="eq:rescaled-descent"></a>$$\label{eq:rescaled-descent}
 \frac{u}{MC_j}
 =\frac1{(M/t_{j,i})z}
 +\frac1{zC_j/C_{j+1}}\frac{h}{MC_{j+1}}.$$ Both $M/t_{j,i}$ and $zC_j/C_{j+1}$ are positive integers. This includes the possible transition from $C_j=C$ to $C_{j+1}=1$, where the second scaling factor is $zC$. If $h=0$, the second term is absent. Otherwise it uses the known expansion for $h\in G_{j+1}$. Consequently every member of $G_j$ has an expansion of length at most $$\label{eq:descent-length}
 B_0\log S+d-j.$$

It remains to prove that almost all integers up to $X_0$ belong to $G_0$. We have built expansions whenever a residue reaches a previously treated numerator; the following count controls all other numerators.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
###### Counting unsuccessful numerators.

Define $$H_j=\{1,\ldots,\lfloor X_j\rfloor\}\setminus G_j,
 \qquad
 \delta_j=\frac{|H_j|}{X_j}.$$ Thus $0\le\delta_j\le1$ and $\delta_d=0$. Fix $j<d$, and abbreviate $X=X_j$, $Y=X_{j+1}=\rho X$. The inclusion $G_{j+1}\subseteq G_j$ gives $$|H_j\cap[1,Y]|\le |H_{j+1}|.$$ Apart from at most $Xe^{-c_*m}$ exceptional integers, every $u\in H_j\cap(Y,X]$ has at least $\rho|I_j|/2$ indices $i$ with $h_{j,t_{j,i}}(u)\le Y$. Each such residue lies in $H_{j+1}$: a residue in $G_{j+1}\cup\{0\}$ would place $u$ in $G_j$.

<a id="eq:bad-pair-count"></a>
For a fixed pair $(h,i)$, every predecessor $u$ satisfies $$u\mid C_jt_{j,i}+h,\qquad 1\le u\le X.$$ There are at most $d_X(C_jt_{j,i}+h)$ such predecessors. Counting pairs with their list indices therefore yields $$\label{eq:bad-pair-count}
 |H_j|\le Xe^{-c_*m}+|H_{j+1}|
 +\frac{2}{\rho|I_j|}
   \sum_{i\in I_j}\ \sum_{h\in H_{j+1}}
       d_X(C_jt_{j,i}+h).$$ Repeated values of $t_{j,i}$ create repeated summands on both sides of this count and require no adjustment. Figure <a href="#fig:predecessors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:predecessors">1</a> shows why the index must be retained in this double count.

<figure id="fig:predecessors">

<figcaption>Schematic of the indexed predecessor count. Summing the right degrees and dividing by the left-degree lower bound gives the last term in <a href="#eq:bad-pair-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bad-pair-count">[eq:bad-pair-count]</a>. The Fourier-exception term $Xe^{−c_{*}m}$ and the inherited term $|H_{j + 1}|$ are counted separately. Equal list values retain different indices. The drawn graph does not specify numerical degrees.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-predecessors"></a>
